MR. CHAIRMAN in the Chair. 
ORAL ANSWERS TO QUESTIONS 
MR.CHAIRMAN:  Now Questions hours. Question no. 151.  
Reducing compliance burden in environmental clearance for MSMEs 
*151.   SHRI DHAIRYASHIL MOHAN PATIL: Will the Minister of MICRO, SMALL AND 
MEDIUM ENTERPRISES be pleased to state:— 
(a)     whether Government is consider ing to allow MSME units in the Konkan 
region of Maharashtra to  meet the 33 per cent green  area requirement for 
environmental clearance collectively, instead of individually for each plot, to ease the 
compliance burden; 
(b)   if so, the details of any gu idelines or pilot models for this; 
(c)   if not, the reasons therefor; and 
(d)  whether recommendations have been  received from industry associations 
regarding cluster-based green compliance? 
THE MINISTER OF MICRO, SMALL AND MEDIUM ENTERPRISES (SHRI JITAN RAM 
MANJHI): (a) to (d) A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) to (d) Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC), 
on the subject ‘Greening/Green Belt Development in Industrial Estates/ 
Parks/Complexes/ Areas, Export Proce ssing Zones (EPZs), Special Economic 
[ 15 December, 2025 ]
25

 
 
Zones (SEZs), Biotech Parks, Leather Comple xes as a whole and in the premises of 
the individual industrial units in the sa id  areas’,  issued  an OM, dated 27 th October, 
2020. As per the OM, an over all green area of at least 33 % of the Industrial Estate 
should be developed and the Industrial Esta te may allocate area for developing green 
cover to respective individual Industrial Units, so as to achieve the overall 33 % Green 
Belt/Green Cover requirements.  
Subsequently vide OM dated 29 th October, 2025, the criteria has been revised 
as below: 
     A. Green Belt/green cover in Indust rial Estates /Parks norms given in the OM 
mandate that in Industrial Estates, a minimum of 10% of the area of the industrial 
estate shall be designated as common gr een area and respect ive red category and 
orange category Industrial units, within the Estate, shall develop 15% and 10% of the 
area of their premises as green belt/green  cover respectively. For green category and 
white category Industrial units within the Es tate, the Green belt requirement has been 
made optional. 
    B.   Green Belt/green cover in Individual Industrial Units outside Industrial Estates 
norms of the OM are reproduced below: 
Category Minimum Green Belt Requirement 
 Predominantly Air Polluting Sectors 
[PlA means air pollution score] 
Other Sectors 
Red 25% (PlA ≥ 80) 20% 
Orange 20%(Pl A ≥ 55) 15% 
Green 10%(Pl A ≥ 25) Optional with no mandatory requirement 
White Optional with no mandatory 
requirement 
 
As per the revised norms,  individual industrial units outside the Industrial 
Estates, in red and orange category industries are required to develop 25% and 20% 
green belt/green cover in their respective premises, which may be reduced by 5%, 
each if they are not predominantly air polluting industries.  
The revised norms provide the benefit of additional land area to all industries, 
including MSMEs across the country, including the State of Maharashtra to expand 
26
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
their operations without compromising the environmental needs of developing green 
belt/ green cover and have eased the compliance burden.

AirSewa web portal and Mobile App  
*152. SHRI MAYANKKUMAR NAYAK: Will the Minister of CIVIL AVIATION be pleased 
to state:- 
[ 15 December, 2025 ]
31

 
 
(a)  the current number of complaints re ceived through the AirSewa web portal 
and mobile app during the last three years; 
(b) whether Government has recently undertaken any assessment of passenger 
satisfaction with the grievance-redressal system of AirSewa; 
(c)   if so, the key findings thereof; 
(d)  whether Government proposes to introduce new features on the portal, 
such as a real- time dashboard or penalt ies for stakeholders causing delays in 
grievance resolution, to enhance transparency and efficiency; and 
(e)    if so, the details thereof? 
THE MINISTER OF CIVIL AVIATION  (SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU) : (a) to 
(e) A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) The year-wise details of complaints  received on the AirSewa web portal 
and mobile application during the last three years are at Annexure. 
(b) Yes, the Ministry regularly monitors  the functioning of AirSewa grievance 
redressal mechanism. Ministry of Civil Av iation undertakes periodic assessment of 
user feedback received on the portal/mobile  application, adherence to prescribed 
timelines for disposal of grievances and quality of redressal. 
(c) The assessments indicate that a sign ificant proportion of grievances are 
being disposed of as per the AirSewa Service Level Agreement (SLA). Overall level of 
passenger satisfaction with the grievance redressal mechan ism has shown an 
improving trend over a period of time, pa rticularly with regard to ease of filing 
complaints and tracking their status. At the same time, certain areas, such as flight 
delays, timely refunds and baggage delivery, have been identified as focus concerns. 
(d) and (e) There is no such specific proposal presently under consideration 
of this Ministry. The AirSewa Portal undergoes continuous technological and 
functional upgradation in compliance with  statutory and operational requirements. 
The most recent enhancements  include introduction of new grievance categories, 
namely "Air Fare" for High Air Fare.

MR. CHAIRMAN:  Now, fourth supplementary, Shri Sandosh Kumar P.   
SHRI SANDOSH KUMAR P:   On 6th of December, 2025, there was an order issued by 
the Civil Aviation Ministry to regulate the air fa res.  Para one states that this order will 
be applicable for travel till th e fares stabilise.  So, what is  meant by the ‘stable price’ 
in air fares?  Secondly, will this order be applicable during Onam, Christmas and 
other seasons also? 
SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU:  Sir, this is a question related to the air fares, 
and especially, when you talk about the su rge in air fares that you see, this is 
generally because it is a deregulated sector  and it is there because of mismatch in 
demand and supply.  When the demand beco mes too high as compared to the seats 
that you can supply in the market, which is wh en the airlines, with their models of air 
pricing, tend to increase the fares.  That  is why, exactly, the hon. Member has also 
mentioned that during Onam or during festiv als, the fares rise.  You observe this not 
only in Kerala but you can observe this in North India during Diwali time also.   
Coming to the capping of airfares on 6 th December, we all are aware of the 
Indigo crisis that has happened, and, we  all know how much disturbance it has 
caused.  One major thing which has happene d during that period is the capacity 
constraint, the capacity at which the Indian aviation was operating.  Indigo is a major 
airline and since their operations were cu rtailed, there was a huge disruption, huge 
cancellation of the regular flights.  So, the capacity has come down and the number 
of seats, which people can use to travel, has come down.  Generally, the other side 
of the trend would be that the airfares woul d rise.  But we have capped the prices to 
keep the airfares at a very reasonable and affordable leve l. Regarding other seasons, 
36
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
that might come up at other times, when th e airfares might rise; the Ministry is closely 
observing all the seasons.  We are continuously engaging… …( Interruptions)…  
MR. CHAIRMAN: No, please.   
SHRI KINJARAPU RAMMOHAN NAIDU : We are continuously engaging with the

Ministry is there to take action.  I can gi ve you other examples also.  During Kumbh 
Mela, we have done it; during Pehalgam incident, we have done it; during Covid also, 
we have done it.  So, whenever there might be a situation that airfares might rise, the 
Ministry will act upon it and take necessary action.

(a)   A s  o f  1 3th November, 2025, the Centra l Water Commission (CWC) is 
monitoring the live storage status of 161 re servoirs across the country and publishes a 
weekly bulletin every Thursday. The total live storage capacity of these monitored 
reservoirs is 182.48 Billion Cubic Metre (B CM), which is about 70.78% of the total 
live storage capacity created through dams in the country.  Further, as per the CWC’s 
bulletin dated 13th November, 2025, the total Live storage available in 161 reservoirs 
was 163.824 BCM compared to 154.171 BC M on the corresponding date i.e. 13 th 
November, 2024. The region-wise storage details as on 13.11.2025 is given at 
Annexure–I . 
(b) and (c)    As per the latest weekly reservoir bulletin dated 04 th December, 
2025, the live storage status in most of th e States is better than their normal storage 
(average storage of last 10 years). Further, as per the India Meteorological 
Department, the country recorded 937.2 mm of rainfall during the southwest 
monsoon season of 2025, which is 108% of  the long period average value of 868.6 
mm. 
As such, No State has reported acute water shortage for Rabi sowing this year. 
Jal Jeevan Mission (JJM) aims towards providing assured drinking water supply 
by functional household tap connection to rural households of the country. 
Pradhan Mantri Krishi Sinchayee Yoja na (PMKSY) aims towards enhancing 
physical access of water on farm, expanding cultivable ar ea under assured irrigation, 
reducing wastage of water and impr oving on-farm water use efficiency.  Funds 
released under PMKSY (under AIBP & CADWM) in the  financial year 2025-26 is 
given at Annexure – II.

Progress status of Ashoknagar oil field after grant of PM
L 
*154. SHRI  SAMIK BHATTACHARYA: Will the Minister of PETROLEUM AND 
NATURAL GAS be pleased to state:- 
(a)  whether all land acquisition for Ashoknagar Oil Field project has been 
completed andthe latest progress made si nce the grant of PML on 24th February, 
2025; 
(b)  whether ONGC has commenced activ ities under the approved integrated 
Field Development Plan (FDP) and the specific milestones achieved so far; 
(c)  expected timeline for commencement of production of commercial oil and 
gas, given that FDP had projected oil within  10 months and gas within 17 months of 
PML deed execution; and 
(d)  steps being taken to ensure that th e deferred production of approximately 
17,922 m³ ofoil envisaged under the Early Development Plan (EDP) is realized without 
further delay? 
THE MINISTER OF PETROLEUM AND NATURAL  GAS (SHRI HARDEEP SINGH 
PURI): (a) to (d) A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) and (b) ONGC had submitted a Pe troleum Mining Lease (PML) request 
to the Government of West Bengal in No vember 2024 with regard to On-land Block 
WB-ONN-2005/4 (Ashoknagar). The State Go vernment issued provisional PML to 
ONGC in February 2025 for an area of 99.06 sq. km. 
Pursuant to this, land measuring 13.49 acres required for drill-site preparation 
for five development wells and associated su rface facilities, has been handed over to 
ONGC on long-term lease.ONGC has comme nced preparatory activities under the 
approved Integrated FDP which, inter-alia, include land arrangements, vendor 
[ 15 December, 2025 ]
45

 
 
shortlisting and tendering for drill-site pr eparation and surface facilities, as well as 
initiation of the process for mobilization of  the drilling rig. These steps constitute key 
milestones achieved post the grant of provisional PML. 
Earlier, ONGC had first reported a discovery Ashoknagar-1 (oil & gas) in the 
WB-ONN-2005/4 block in West Bengal in 20 18. This was the outcome of continuous 
exploration efforts of ONGC in the Bengal Sedimentary basin over five decades. 
ONGC has invested about Rs.1000 crore till now in this block for exploration activities.  
An Early Development Plan (EDP) wa s approved in 2020 which enabled 
extended oil testing from Ashoknagar-1, pr oducing 289.74 MT till July 2022. After 
approval of EDP, Petroleum Mining Leas e (PML) was requested in 2020, which was 
not received from the State Government, t hus, commercial production (except test 
production) of 17922 m3 basis EDP could not be undertaken.  
As ONGC had an ongoing Petroleum Exploration License (PEL), they 
continued exploratory drillingsin this block which confirmed additional discoveries, 
Kankpul-1 (gas, 2022) and Ranaghat-2 (gas , 2024), leading to an Integrated Field 
Development Plan (IFDP) approval in Ju ly 2024. The IFDP subsumed the proposals 
under the EDP. 
 (c) and (d) After receiving provisional PML, necessary documents for 
signing of Lease Deed have been submitte d to the Government of West Bengal in 
May 2025. The approval of Lease Deed is still awaited from the State Government. 
Only upon signing of the L ease Deed, production from the block as per the IFDP can 
be commenced and then the production can be eventually ramped up.  
 On 10 December 2025, the Government of  West Bengal have issued a letter 
to ONGC regarding computation of stamp duty basis the lease rent or the annual 
royalty, which-ever is higher. ONGC has submitted their reply to the State 
Government on 12 December 2025 seeking the levy of Stamp Duty in accordance with 
the provisions under recently notified Pe troleum & Natural Gas Rules, 2025 (PNG 
Rules) wherein the rent is to be paid for th e entire term of the l ease and that the rent 
is the consideration for the petroleum lease. Sub-rule 4 under Rule 6 of the PNG 
Rules provides that the value of the lease sh all be the total lease rent payable for its 
term.  
 In addition to the rent fixed under th e PNG Rules, Royalty shall, in any case, 
accrue to the State in on-land blocks on basis of production from the block.

[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
 
SHRI SAMIK BHATTACHARYA:  Sir, my question is related to Ashoknagar Oil Field in 
West Bengal where the Government of Indi a has already invested more than Rs.1,000 
crore.  Sir, through you, I want to know about this from the hon. Minister.  Can the 
hon. Minister confirm whether the recent issue was raised by the Government of West 
Bengal on stamp duty computation despite a clear provision under the Petroleum and 
Natural Gas Rules, 2025?   Can any furthe r delay in production compromise India’s 
energy security?   
SHRI HARDEEP SINGH PURI:  Sir, I would like to thank the hon. Member not only for 
this specific question, which I am happy to answer, but al so for his persistence on 
this issue.  Projects related to Explor ation and Production (E&P) are, by very 
definition, complex.  They have a long gest ation period and they involve heavy capital 
expenditure.  Insofar as the Ashoknagar Proj ect is concerned, in this House, some of 
us, at least, have been very familiar with this.  The production sharing contract was 
signed for exploration Block WB-ONN-200 5/4 between ONGC and OIL during the 
NELP Round VII.  This was done in 2008 and ONGC was the contractor for this Block.  
This Block is located in North 24 Parganas District and the di scovery was made in 
2018. As the hon. Member has said, it is true that ONGC has spent Rs. 1,000 crore 
already on it. It is our estimate that the Bengal basin contains a total of 240 million 
barrels of oil equivalent proven reserves in place. If you go by this figure, at 30 per 
cent recovery, the cumulative production va lue can be around Rs. 45,000 crore. Out 
of which, the State of Bengal would get Rs. 4,500 crore. The limited point I am 
seeking to make here is that initially a lot of time is taken and there is long gestation 
and heavy capital expenditure, but once you begin to see the oil and gas being 
extracted, it is pure joy after that because that expenditure is recovered.  Now, the 
operator applied for a Petroleum Mining Leas e to the Government of West Bengal in 
2020. The provisional PML -- Petroleum Mini ng Lease -- was received from the West 
Bengal Government in February, 2025. I have had the opportunity in July this year of 
convening a meeting of all the States along with all the other stakeholders, and I am 
very happy to say that there is a new en thusiasm, a new spirit  of cooperation and 
everything is moving ahead. I can understand the hon. Member's anxiety of whether 
we are going to get delayed further. The exchange of letters between ONGC and the 
West Bengal Government has only taken pl ace on December 10 and 12. I have copies 
of the letters with me. My reading, based on where we have reached so far, in the 
light of the latest legislation which we ha ve, -- the oilfield development rules has 
come about after that date; I think, it was notified only about 5-6 days ago -- I don't 
[ 15 December, 2025 ]
47

 
 
see any problem. The situation is quite clear  that stamp duty has to be paid on the 
duration of the entire lease. So, if the lease in this case is 30 years, the stamp duty will 
be paid on the entire lease period. The issue is about royalty. Stamp duty will be paid 
on royalty as and when the royalty is rece ived. I mean, the whole idea is to facilitate 
both the concerned State Government and the operator and, I think, the hon. 
Member, who has been pursuing  this, will also realise th at there has been all-round 
movement on this. Personally, I am not a soothsayer and I don't like to anticipate. 
But, I think, considering when we started -- it has been going on for 50 years – in 
2008, we had the production sharing agreement; in 2018, we moved forward on PML; 
now we have everything in place; maybe, another few weeks, and hopefully, we can 
say that this is something that we have achieved and that production will go on.  
Sir, I am very happy to tell you that States, when they cooperate with the 
exploration and production activity, get revenue. They get revenues from petroleum 
and diesel. I can just share wi th you that the State of An dhra Pradesh is getting Rs. 
16,000 crore, Gujarat is getting Rs. 24,500  crore, Maharashtra is getting Rs. 37,000 
crore, Rajasthan is getting Rs. 18,900 crore, Tamil Nadu is getting Rs. 24,861 crore, 
Uttar Pradesh is getting Rs. 31,200 crore and West Bengal is getting Rs. 13,000 crore. 
I am sure once this is fructified and we have other discoveries, it would increase. 
Now, an amount of Rs. 1,000 crore has been  invested and an area of 99 square 
kilometres is there. This is  only one small area when th i s  b e g a n .  I  a m  s u r e  W e s t  
Bengal's revenues will also go up as a result  of this. So, I am r easonably confident. 
As I said, based on the latest law, stamp duty on lease rent and stamp duty on royalty 
will be payable as and when the extraction takes place and the royalty, as a fixed 
amount, accrues to the State Government.

MR. CHAIRMAN: Minister is giving the reply ...(Interruptions) ... 
SHRI HARDEEP SINGH PURI: Sir, I think, in a normal seminar kind of discussion, this 
is a very valid question. But, the thrust of my reply is, I think, we have already reached 
48
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
the point where this deal is going to be fi nalised based on the exchange of letters on 
10th and 12 th December. However, I think, the hon. Member deserves to be 
commended for asking a question which is  overall, namely, if States do not 
cooperate, do they or do they not under mine the country's national economic and 
energy security! That is a general point.  Le t me just say that I am particularly worried 
because I see a situation where we are produc ing, let us say, a total of 55,000 barrels 
a day from the KG basin, another 100,000 barrels from Rajasthan -- these are rough 
figures in my mind -- 100,000 barrels from Assam and, ma ybe, 1 lakh more barrels 
from Gujarat.  These are approximate figures.   It is precisely for that reason that I 
convened an Urja Manthan with partnershi ps of the States where these discussions 
have taken place and, as a result of whic h, a large number of States have given

So, if States withhold clearances, my submis sion, through you, to the States is, they 
are undermining their own economic well-being.   I just read out how much States are 
earning from the revenue on petrol and di esel and it would be  very clear that the 
States which have been slow would now have to recover.  Some States are doing well 
but, overall, we have an economy growing at 8.2 per cent in the last quarter. Our 
energy consumption, according to  the International Energy A ssociation, is that in the 
next 20 years, we would be consuming mo re than 30 per cent of the incremental 
demand. So, the energy consumption is ri sing. Our imports are also rising even 
though domestic production is going up.  But, today, we are consuming 5.6 million 
barrels of crude a day. Our refining capacity is going up. I would have our State 
Governments, in partnership with the Cent ral Government, to get more and more of 
this revenue.  And, I think, what the ho n. Member has asked is valid but let the 
message go through this House that the St ates actually serve their own economic 
well-being and the welfare of their citi zens when they grant these licenses 
expeditiously.  I am also confident that  with E&P going up, we have the next 10 th 
round of bids going to come up very shortly. Overall, with the new law in place, all the 
stakeholders would benefit from the far-reaching changes th at the Government, 
under the hon. Prime Minister, has made in the last few years.

DR. FAUZIA KHAN :  Sir, the Lok Sabha reply dated 13 th May, 2025 confirms that

delay will affect the earlier realization of the 17,922 M 3 of oil envisaged under the FDP

MR. CHAIRMAN:  Hon. Minister. 
SHRI HARDEEP SINGH PURI :  Sir, I want to thank the hon. Member for referring to 
those timelines.  She herself would have noted, that was a provisional petroleum 
mining lease.  The Ministry concerned is not the Mining Ministry.  Earlier, in 1948, we 
had a law which governed both  mining, that is, extraction of ores and petroleum 
mining.  But this latest Act that we brou ght, separates the two. Now, a petroleum 
mining lease has to be negotiated by the concerned State Government or other 
entities with the Central Government and th e timeline starts only when the lease deed 
is signed. So, when the hon. Member refers to say 10 months or 15 months, that is a 
timeline from the date it starts because we are reasonably certain that the oil and gas 
are waiting to be extracted.  But unless it is granted and signed, the latest step in that 
direction, I want to remind the hon. Member , is that there has been an exchange of 
letters on 10
th and 12th of December, just barely three-four days ago.  There, the State 
Government had referred to wanting, I think, stamp duty both on the lease period and 
the royalty.  I want it to be pointed out th at the latest law clearly stipulates, without 
any degree of ambiguity, that the lease stamp duty, which is a very small amount, 
would be paid for the entire duration. But,  the stamp duty on revenue earned from oil 
extracted, as a percentage, I mentioned how much we have estimated will be paid as 
the extraction goes on.  So, I am very clear that  timelines have not slipped. It is just 
that if the petroleum mining lease deed ha d been signed, say two months earlier, it 
would have started from there.  But, I am reasonably confident with these 
clarifications and the on-going interaction between the ONGC and the State 
Government of West Bengal, and I can tell you the same thing is happening with the 
other State Governments. The new law brings  in a degree of clarity which leaves 
everyone clear and certain.  It brings in pred ictability and, I think,  all these leases will 
now go forward.

SHRI YERRAM VENKATA SUBBA REDDY (Andhra Pradesh) : Thank you very much, 
Sir, for giving me this opportunity. Electoral  reforms do not mean that you bring some 
changes after Lok Sabha elections and co nclude that you have done electoral 
reforms.  We have to bring reforms at the State level and also at the national level. 
And, only such electoral reforms will protect and preserve democracy and the 
democratic process in the country. 
We all know that electoral reform is  a continuous process. The Election 
Commission has to learn from its discrepa ncies and mistakes;  mistakes committed 
d u r i n g  A s s e m b l y  e l e c t i o n s ,  b e  i t  i n  A ndhra Pradesh or anyw here else, mistakes 
committed in the General Elections or ev en mistakes committed during panchayat 
and local body elections. 
I am representing Andhra Pradesh State in this House. So, whatever examples 
I give here, I will give only relating to my State, pertaining to the 2024 General 
56
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
Elections. I am not casting any aspersions on  anybody or any party. But, I am only 
quoting the facts and suggesting what is to be done by the Election Commission to 
maintain its integrity and transparency an d address shortcomings . So, I will be brief 
and pointed in my submissions. 
First, I will come to Form 20. It contai ns the exact polled vote data, excluding 
postal ballots. The Election Commission issued a Press Note on 25th May 2024, 
indicating the absolute number of voters wh o participated in the first phase of the 
2024 General Elections, which also includes th e fourth phase elections held in Andhra 
Pradesh. The exact number of votes polled has been shared with polling agents 
through Form 17C. This Press Note is said to be absolute and final. But, there is a 
huge discrepancy in the final votes counted and the exact polled votes, declared by 
the Election Commission of India through Fo rm 20.  The discrepancy occurred in 25 
constituencies, that means all 25 parliament ary constituencies in  Andhra Pradesh. I 
suggest for the consideration of the Election Commission, through you, to look into 
why this discrepancy has happened, who is responsible for this, and what action it 
has taken against the erring officials. 
M y  s e c o n d  p o i n t ,  I  w i s h  t o  m a k e ,  i s  r elating to Form 17A. It deals with the 
Voters’ Register. Simultaneous elections fo r Lok Sabha and Assembly were held in 
some States, including Andhra Pradesh.  We have a common voter list. We have 
some polling stations, and polling is simultaneous. In such a scenario, the votes 
registered in Form 17A, the accounts of th e votes recorded in Form 17C, and votes 
entered in EVM should be the same for both Lok Sabha and Assembly elections. And, 
if there is any discrepancy, that has to be  recorded in Form 17A under Rule No. 49O. 
So, if there is any mismatch between Lo k Sabha and Assembly votes, it raises 
procedural and legal concerns. But such la pses were observed in the 2024 General 
Elections in Andhra Pradesh. This indi cates that voters’ data was altered or 
manipulated, or there was a deviation in the electoral procedure. There are many 
examples in many constituencies -- Raptad u, Dharmavaram, Rayadurga, Tadipathri, 
Allagadda, Kadapa, Pulivendula, etc. Like this, in so many constituencies, there were 
a lot of discrepancies.  So, I appeal to the Election Commissioner to conduct an 
enquiry as to why it had happened in order to  ensure that such things do not occur in 
future. All I want, the YSR Congress Party wa nts, and the people of Andhra Pradesh 
want, is that there should be fairness in th e conduct of elections, clarity in outcomes, 
and transparency in public disclosure. 
[ 15 December, 2025 ]
57

 
 
So, through you, Sir, I would like to put forth my suggestions, my party’s 
suggestions, to the hon. Prime Minister, to the Government, and to the Election 
Commission.  (a)  Mandatory public release of CCTV footage,  Form 17A, and 17-C., 
and Presiding Officer's Diaries;  (b) statutory enforcement of Supreme Court-
mandated EVM’s verification; (c)  the most important of them all is to replace EVMs 
with paper ballots in all future elections, because the electoral system that cannot be 
audited cannot command trust. EVMs may be efficient, but cannot be trusted. Paper 
ballot may not be efficient, but can be trusted. You need trust in democracy; and, 
(d) live streaming or 100 per cent webcasting at all polling stations. 
With these submissions, Sir, I conclude by just saying that the YSR Congress 
Party has brought this to the notice of the Election Commission, not once or twice, 
but multiple times. The YSR Congress Party had got a 40 per cent vote share in the 
last General Elections held in 2024. And, the mismatch that we have mentioned above

Jawaharlal Nehru was Prime Minister.  Everybody knows what happened in 1964; I do 
not wish to state that.  After the demise of Pandit Jawaharlal Nehru, the tallest leader, 
Shri Lal Bahadur Shastri, became Prime Mini ster.  He was there for a short period.

became Prime Minister.

three or four elections, Madam Gandhi was able to score in the election. When there 
was a decline in the number of candidates,  then, she thought of bifurcating the 
Parliament and the Assembly separately to fight the election. Even then, I fought the 
election. I never raised any objection.  I was in the opposition. Earlier, there was 
Samata Party and Janata Dal. I was the Pres ident of Janata Dal when Janata Dal was 
formed during Morarji Desai.  I never rais ed any objection about the bifurcation of 
Parliament and Assembly elections. Even then, we bowed our heads and fought 
elections. I was defeated once in the Asse mbly. I won the Assembly seven times. I 
won Parliament four times and I was defeat ed three times by the Congress. I never 
blamed the Congress for defeating my by manipulating the voters’ list.

speak about the health of a democracy.  It  is a legacy that was handed to us not 
easily, but the one that was forged in the fire of sacrifice by our freedom fighters.  It is 
the solemn promise of our Constitution that every citizen remains a true master of this 
Republic. Talking about elector al reforms and the power of  the ballot, I would take 
this honour to present before this House our own experiences from Telangana.  
Sir, the power of the ballot was clearly demonstrated to the nation by our 
leader KCRji when the struggle for Telang ana began. Our struggle for Telangana for 
the last 14 years saw unprecedented kind of dharnas. Women, farmers, students, 
employees and every person in the society of Telangana fought for it, but the only 
important thing which could awaken the Gove rnment of India and which could bring a 
sense of responsibility was the power of ba llot. To quote the power of the ballot, my 
leader KCRji, to demonstrate that the will of the people is so clearly visible, resigned 
twice as Member of Parliament. He resigned twice from different regions of Telangana 
so that the country gets a sense that it is not one particular region or one particular 
area. He resigned when he was not in po wer. Our party was neither ruling Telangana 
nor at the Centre, but the courage of KCRji,  the trust and belief he had on ballot was 
visibly demonstrated. It is not only him, seeing his resignation, the people of 
Telangana compelled leaders of other polit ical parties to resign as that would 
demonstrate the kind of will of the people which was prevailing there. So, this being 
the power of the voter, my leader and my pa rty are very keen that every eligible voter 
of Telangana and every eligible citizen of this country should be enrolled as a voter.   
Sir, the debate has been continuing fo r the last two days. We support and say 
that electoral reforms are, definitely, a b ackbone for a healthy democracy. But, to 
ensure this, the voter list sh ould be accurate, inclusive an d transparent. A large and 
diverse nation like ours needs clean electoral  rolls. We totally agre e with that. Having 
said that, we have to ensure registration and updation to facilitate voting access to 
migrants, disabled, senior citizens, urban poor and people with limited 
documentation should be entitled to their vo ting rights. Strict re-verification should 
not become a mechanism for exclusion.  Ensure that no eligible voter loses his right to 
vote simply because accessing paperwork is  difficult. Today, there are initiatives by 
every State Government and Government of  India, like ease of doing business, ease 
of communicating, etc. We have seen peop le through MeeSeva in Telangana getting 
their certificates electronically. In an age lik e this, why is it a challenge for a person 
whose name has been removed from the elect oral roll to get back into the electoral 
roll when he is an entitled citizen of this country? That should be the concern and that 
should be the mechanism to be adopted. So, while the co ncern definitely is on the

voters’ exclusion, but then we should  also be equally concerned about the

We have seen in local bodies.  I wa s reading about a model in Gujarat --

defection. So, that has becoming compellin g now.  Of course, why allow the court,

THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. SASMIT PATRA) :  Shri Praful Patel – not present.   Shri 
Gurvinder Singh Oberoi. Congratulations!  This is your maiden speech. 
SHRI GURWINDER SINGH OBEROI (Jammu and Kashmir) :  Hon. Vice-Chairman, 
Sir, and respected Members, I rise today with humility and gratitude to my party 
leadership, Dr. Farooq Abdulla and hon. Chief Minister, Shri Omar Abdullah, my party 
colleagues and the people of Jammu & Kashmir, whom I represent in this House. 
 Sir, I am the first Kashmiri Sikh to ha ve come to the Rajya Sabha.  That speaks 
volumes of the secularism of my Party, my  Party leadership and the people of Jammu 
and Kashmir.  In Jammu and Kashmir, our faiths may be different, our religions may 
be different, but it is humanity and brotherhood that bind us together.   
Sir, I express my strong reservations on the so called SIR-electoral reforms.  I 
say ‘so-called’ because what is being presen ted as reforms is actually restructuring 
democracy to suit the people in power. Th e Government tells us that these reforms 
are necessary to clean the electoral system.  But the reality is different. There is no 
national debate; no expert Commission and draft prepared in secrecy is brought here 
for a hurried approval.  This is how electoral  reforms are done.  This is how political 
engineering is done.  If this electoral re forms become law, the implications will be 
dangerous and far-reaching.  The Election Commission risks being weakened, 
centralized and placed under indirect executi ve influence. Provisions of funding, 
technology and campaign regulations will hit small and regional parties like us. Rapid 
digitisation of key electoral process with out independent audit raises the fear of 
manipulations, surveillance and targeting.   Reforms without consensus do not 
strengthen democracy. They fracture the very confidence in the very process that 
elects the Governments. Therefore, this ex ercise has, unfortunately, raised serious 
concerns for decades.  Electoral rolls were revised with integrity, transparency and 
fairness. Institutions were respected and pr ocesses were sacrosanct. But, today, we 
are forced to ask: Are these institutions being compromised? Are age-old procedures 
being questioned only to create confusion and delay? Is the trust between the people 
and the system being weakened deliberately? This is not about names on a list; this is 
not about the soul, this is about the soul of our democracy. If electoral rolls are 
manipulated, democracy itself is manipulated.  If institutions bend, the will of the 
people breaks. Let us be cl ear. Many years from now, ma ny of us may not be here, 
but the people of the great nation will remember only one thing,  who stood with the 
Constitution and who stood against it. Our forefathers conceived a Constitution so 
beautiful, so inclusive, that no matter whether we come from Kanyakumari or 
80
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
Kashmir, we all were treated equally.  That was their vision; this is our responsibility. 
Today, one party is in power, tomorrow another will be; Governments change, the 
nation remains.  But the question histo ry will ask is simple: When the foundations 
were shaken, who defended the Republic? We  must say this loudly and clearly, the 
SIR process inspires suspicion. Let thos e who think electoral processes can be 
twisted, know that the voter may be silent, but the voter is never helpless. Those who 
try to erase the names from rolls, understand, you cannot erase the people's will, you 
cannot delete democracy.

the thumb of the Executive.  I do want to confess that this is a very important question 
and it has given me a great deal of headache. ” T h a t  i s  B a b a  Saheb Ambedkar for

SHRI S. NIRANJAN REDDY (Andhra Pradesh) : Thank you Mr. Deputy Chairman Sir 
for giving me the opportunity.  Sir, the subj ect being electoral reforms discussion that 
is happening, I wanted to make a few subm issions without reference to any political 
undertones.  There has been a lot of discussion with regard to the Special Intensive 
Revision. As a Member, I feel that the Special  Intensive Revision is legally sanctioned.  
142
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
We have Article 324 that enables the Election Commission to undertake 
superintendence. Sir, we have Section 21(3) of the 1950 Act which empowers the 
Election Commission to call for these revisions, special or intensive.  We have Rule 25 
of the Registration of Elect ors Rules, 1960, which once again empowers the Election 
Commission to undertake such revisions. If there is a legal provision available for the 
Election Commission to do this, the next ques tion would be:  Is there a necessity to 
do this? Sir, it is, may be, undisputable that  our electoral rolls are far from perfect. A 
survey conducted in the year 1999 by a noted NGO, an organization, called Lok Satta, 
found that in urban constituencies, 44.5 pe r cent of voters do not exist; in rural 
constituencies, it is 15 per cent.  I recognize that the in cidence may have come down 
from what it was found in 1999. But, Bihar throws up a very interesting statistic. Sir, 
like many other Members, I was very apprehen sive when they announced that on the 
eve of elections they would be undertaking th is SIR exercise. I felt whether there is a 
political reason behind the SIR being undertak en here. Sir, the even ts that transpired 
bear out that after the Bihar SI R exercise that has been carri ed out, it is found that 65 
lakh voters’ names were registered on th e rolls incorrectly. Now, this matter was 
taken to the Supreme Court on apprehen sion. The Supreme Court entered certain 
procedural caveats. The Supreme Court said, if you are taking out anybody's name, 
give them notice. Sir, as far as I see, because the matter is still sub judice before the 
Supreme Court, after the exer cise has been completed, after 65 lakh voters’ names 
have been deleted, there is a very interesting Press statem ent that has been released 
by the Election Commission of India, whic h have corroborated with the hearing that 
happened in the Supreme Court also.  The El ection Commission, in its Press note of  
17th November, -- I will place this for au thentication -- has said something very 
interesting. It said that after all these pe ople have been removed, there have been 
zero appeals against wrong inclusion  or  exclusion of any elector after Special 
Intensive Revision in Bihar in any of the 38  districts, from any of the electors and, 
most importantly, from any of the 12 rec ognized political parties. The Supreme Court 
was mindful that certain people whose na mes have been removed may not have a 
chance of filing an appeal.  So, it directed the Legal Services Authority to depute para 
legal persons for the purpose of assist ing anyone whose names may have been 
wrongly deleted. Sir, it was noted by the Supreme Court at the hearing on 26 th  
November, 2025, that no person has come forward to say  that  they have been

SHRI MANAS RANJAN MANGARAJ (Odisha) : Sir, I rise to speak on the Election 
Reforms, with specific reference to the Spec ial Intensive Revision (SIR) of the Voter 
List.  No one opposes the need of a clean  and accurate electoral roll, but the manner 
in which the SIR is being implemented toda y has created serious concerns across the 
country.  The ground reports show that ge nuine voters are being asked to produce 
multiple documents.  Poor citizens, migrant workers, daily wage earners and people 
in remote and border areas are facing the greatest hardship.  When eligible citizens 
fear deletion from the voter list, it is no longer reform; it is exclusion.   
 Sir, the timing of this exercise is deeply troubling.  Conducting such a n 
intensive revision just before Assembly Elections in several Stat es has created doubt 
and mistrust.  Electoral reforms must inspire confidence, not suspicion.  There is still 
no clear answer to a fundamenta l question - is SIR only a vo ter list revision, or, is it 
turning into citizenship verification? The lack of clear communication from the Election 
Commission has created unnecessary anxiety.  Silence on such a sensitive issue is 
unacceptable.  Therefore, I demand immedi ate corrective action.  Clear and uniform 
guidelines must be issued.  Documentat ion requirements must be simplified.  
Adequate time must be given.  Technology must assist voters, not intimidate them.

Ǜी हषर् वधर्न Ǜृंगला (नामिनदȃिशत):  माननीय उपसभापित महोदय, आज electoral reforms के

reforms आम बदलाव नहीं हȅ, बिÊक दुिनया का सबसे बड़े लोकतंतर् को मजबूत करने का एक 
बड़ा अिभयान है।  जैसे हमारे माननीय Ģधान मंतर्ी नरेन्दर् मोदी जी हमȂ अक्सर याद िदलाते हȅ, नए

Sir, as someone, who has represente d his country in different geographies 
across the world, I can say wi th some confidence that our efforts at electoral reforms 
are not isolated.  Progressive democracies across the world are engaged in a similar 
exercise of ongoing and regular electoral reforms.  They do it for a number of 
purposes.  One, of course, is the change.  The requirements of the 21 st century are 
different from those of the 20 th century.  The other purpose is to increase efficiency, 
and most important of all, is to enhance accountability to the people.   
[THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (SHRI BHUBANESWAR KALITA) in the Chair.] 
 Sir, it is in this very endeavour that  the hon. Prime Minister has also placed 
before the nation the vision of holding si multaneous elections through ‘One Nation, 
One Election’ initiative.  This aims to bring stability, reduce expenditure and make our 
democracy more efficient and future ready.  Perhaps, the hon. Members are aware 
that our country conducted simultaneous elections of the Lok Sabha and the State 
Assemblies regularly until 1967.   Over the decades, frag mented election schedules 
have led to constant campaign mode, politi cal paralysis and colossal expenditure.  A 
high-level Committee, chaired by the former  President, Shri Ram Nath Kovind, was 
constituted in 2023 to chart the roadmap for simultaneous polls.                                                            
[ 15 December, 2025 ]
149

 
 
This Committee conducted 65 meetings with stakeholders, including all major 
parties, legal experts including former Chie f Justices, industry bodies and citizens.  
Notably, the Committee invited 21,000 public  suggestions and 81 per cent of those 
responses affirmed support for simultaneous polls.  Previously, the Law Commission, 
in its 1999 Report, had earlier suggested simu ltaneous elections every five years.  A 
2015 Parliamentary Committee also examined phasing in joint polls.  I can tell you that 
the concept of a single vote cy cle for elections at different levels of the Government is 
practiced in a number of democracies across the world.  Sir, take the example of 
Sweden, a country which holds simultaneous elections in every electoral cycle of four 
years on the same date for its General el ections, for County elections and Municipal 
elections.  As a result of th is, the voter turnout is over 80 per cent because voters are 
only voting once in four years and they  understand the importance of that vote. 
Compared to that, our voter turnout at 65  per cent is well below this level. Other 
countries that also follow a One Nation One Election system are: South Africa, 
Indonesia, Belgium and the European Par liament.  There are many countries that 
follow a system which has synchronized electi ons at different levels, State level, etc. 
but may not be exactly all elections.  
Sir, another reform is One Nation One Voter List, which has to be seen in 
conjunction with One Nation One Election.  Currently in many states, the voters' list 
for panchayat and municipal elections is maintained separately by State Election 
Commissions, often duplicating the rolls pr epared by the Election Commission of 
India for Lok Sabha and Assembly elections .  A common electoral role was endorsed 
by the Cabinet in principle.  One Nation On e Voter List will save enormous effort and 
public money while improv ing accuracy.  In fact, th e Law Commission in 2015 
supported a single Voters' List to streamline the process.   
सर, चुनाव हमारे लोकतंतर् का सबसे बड़ा त्योहार है।  To keep this festival pure and 
transparent, our systems must evolve over  time. This Government has consistently 
working to make governance modern, effici ent and citizen-centric.  The reforms we 
are discussing today continue in that direction. The Government has taken firm steps 
to identify and curb illegal infiltration and to ensure that only legitimate citizens 
participate in our democratic process. The re forms before us strengthen that resolve.  
They enhance coordination, improve verifica tion mechanisms and close loopholes to 
make our electoral processes cleaner, fairer and more accessible.  
Sir, hon. Prime Minister is of the view that strong democracies are built on 
strong institutions.  Through these re forms, we are strengthening those very 
150
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
institutions that safeguard the voice of ever y citizen.  The purpose of the SIR process 
is to make India for Indians and cleanse out illegal immigrants in the country. It will 
empower the honest voter, discourage ma lpractice and ensure that no one can 
question the fairness of our elections.  We  want young voters to feel encouraged, 
senior citizens to feel support ed and every citizen to feel that their vote truly counts. 
These reforms are a step towards that vision  and the entire nation is supporting this 
exercise with sincerity. However, the persis tent obstruction by some political parties 
and refusal to cooperate raise an unavoidable question as to why a simple transparent 
verification process is being opposed and what are the forces behind this resistance. 
Sir, the Supreme Court of India has taken serious note of issues pertaining to 
obstruction of SIR in West Bengal. In December 2025, the Supreme Court sharply 
criticized reports of local intimidation and non-cooperation in the SIR process, 
particularly, pointing to West Bengal.  The Court warned that continued obstruction of 
the roll revision could easily cause anarchy.  The court also affirmed the Election 
Commission's constitutional power to carry out this important exercise.

SHRI UJJWAL DEORAO NIKAM (Nominated) : Sir, I rise to speak on the Electoral 
Reforms. Indeed, I would like to thank the hon. Chairman, hon. Leader of the House 
and hon. Parliamentary Affairs Minister for their magnanimity in allowing discussion on 
Election Reforms. This is, particularly, significant because as per Rule 238, a Member 
while speaking shall not refer to any fact on which judicial decision is pending. I would 
like to inform this House, and we all are aware, that the subject matter of SIR is 
pending before the Supreme Court. Yet, keeping in mind the sentiments of the 
Opposition, the Government has agreed to ho ld a discussion on it. This is called the 
healthy spirit of the Government.  
Sir, over the past decade, under the dynamic leadership of our hon. Prime 
Minister, Shri Narendra Modi, our country has undertaken a transf ormative journey, 
marked by transparency, accountability and an unwavering commitment to the rule of 
law. SIR is another significan t step in this ongoing process. Having spent more than 
four decades in the legal profession and having observed the justice system very 
closely, I can say with full responsibility that electoral reforms like SIR addresses 
several long-standing procedural gaps that have often led to ambiguity, delays and 
inconsistency. We are discussing electoral re forms. But let us be brutally honest; this 
discussion, coming from the benches oppo site, reeks of hypocrisy. When Congress 
was ruling and were in power, they treated electoral reform reports not as blueprints 
for action, but as coasters for their evening tea. Now, let us look at certain 
recommendations which were made during their regime. Hon. Law Minister has made 
a reference to two such recommendations. The first recommendation which he 
referred to was the Tarkunde Committee Report of 1975. What happened to it? It was 
thrown in the dustbin. Then, the seco nd committee report; the Dinesh Goswami 
Committee of 1990 recommended that the Election Commission be given teeth to 
punish booth-capturing, but it was ignored. The third important Committee was the 
Indrajit Gupta Committee of 1998 on State Fun ding of Elections; they sat on it. The 
recommendations of these Committees were ma de as graveyard. Why? It is because 
status quo  suited them – a system opaque enough to manipulate, yet chaotic 
enough to claim plausible deniability. 
Sir, let me remind them as to how they  systematically diluted the power of the 
Election Commission. When T. N. Seshan began to enforce the Model Code of 
Conduct in the early 1990s, stopping mi suse of the Government machinery and 
ending the era of booth-capturing, they di d not applaud him. They panicked. On the 
contrary, they cut down hi s powers also. It was not the Parliament, under their 
leadership, that cleaned up the system; it was the cour ts, we, the lawyers and the 
154
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
judiciary, had to step in where they had failed. The Supreme Court in Union of India 
versus Association for Democratic Reforms forced candidates to disclose their 
criminal antecedents. The Government of th e day did not want you to know who was 
voting for you. In Lilly Thomas versus Union of India, the Supreme Court held that 
convicted MPs and MLAs were to be disqualified immediately. What was the instinct 
of this opposition then? They tried to brin g an Ordinance to overturn that judgment 
and protect convicted criminals. Today, one of the hon. Members of DMK and also 
the Congress Party raised a question on EVMs in this august House. I, being a 
lawyer, dealing with evidence, let me tell my  brother colleagues that in a recent 2024 
judgment in Association for Democratic Reforms versus Election Commission of India, 
the Supreme Court did not just dismiss thei r doubts on EVMs; th ey dismantled them. 
The court explicitly ruled that a return to paper ballot is regressive, yet they cling on to 
this narrative. You want this country to go back to the era of ballot stuffing; the era 
where muscle power determined the vote co unt, not the voter. The last meaningful 
General Election the Congress Party won wa s in 2009. The electorate then was 
roughly 717 million. Today, in 2024-25, our Government is managing an electorate of 
nearly 980 million people. 
This is a jump over 250 million voters - more than the entire population of 
Brazil, added to our rolls.  To suggest th at we manually count 970 million paper 
ballots, is not just logistical illiteracy, it is  an invitation to anarchy.  It would take 
months to count.  The litigation alone would paralyze the democracy.  In 2009, you

recording a verdict you do not like.  The Government today acts on the Law 
Commission's 170
th report. The Government wants “One Nation, One Election ” to

duplicate voters, ghost voters that the O pposition relied on for the decades. An 
accurate roll, we know, is the basic foundatio n of the Constitution.  Section 21 of the 
Representation of the Peoples Act author izes the Election Commission to undertake 
special revision of voters.  This revision has many advantages. I do not want to quote 
again. Many speakers have already said this. In this process, millions of the 
grassroots Government personnel are involved.  If they start questioning the electoral 
processes, then, we are questioning the in tegrity of the millions of the Government 
servants.  Clean electoral rolls increas e public trust, enhance fairness, and 
significantly reduce opportunities for manipu lation or misuse. I would therefore say, 
the SIR is serving as a surgical strike ag ainst bogus and duplicate beneficiaries, who 
have been looting Government schemes like PM Kisan Yojana, as well as the

SHRI P.P. SUNEER (Kerala) : Sir, it is imperative that this House discusses and 
brings forward Electoral Reforms to protect the democratic cont ent of our Republic. 
Elections are not a ritual; th ey are the very m echanism through which the will of the 
people is translated into governance. Any dist ortion in this process strikes at the root 
of parliamentary democracy, that is, representation.  
 At the outset, I must draw the attentio n of this House to the grave  threat 
posed by the on-going Special Intensive Revisi on of electoral rolls. What should have 
been a transparent and citizen-friendly exercise has instead become an ordeal.  In 
Bihar, lakhs of votes were deleted during the process and, shockingly, over three lakh 
voters were added after the Election Commi ssion declared the final electoral roll on 
30th September 2025. This alone raises seriou s questions about the credibility, intent 
and scientific basis of this exercise.  
Across several states, SIR has become a nightmare for citizens and election 
workers alike. Ordinary voters are being co mpelled to travel long distances, produce 
excessive documentation, and live under consta nt anxiety as if their right to vote is a 
privilege to be earned, not a constitu tional guarantee. At the same time,  booth-level 
officers and field workers are under unprecedented pressure due to vague, 
unscientific and hurried guidelines. Re ports of mental stress and extreme 
[ 15 December, 2025 ]
157

 
 
consequences among workers are deeply dist urbing. The entire responsibility for this 
chaos lies with the Election Commission of India.  
A level-playing field is the soul of fr ee and fair elections. When the referee 
appears partisan, the match losses legitimacy. Whether it is selective enforcement of 
rules, silence on glaring violations or op aque decision-making, the credibility of the 
Election Commission is under strain and this is dangerous for our democracy.  
To address three of these challenges, my  party, the CPI, places the following 
demands on record of this House. The firs t one--Curb the role of money in politics 
through strict enforcement of expenditure limits and by introducing State funding of 
elections as recommended by the Indrajit Gupta Committee. This will enable people 
from poorer sections, the majority of our country, to meaningfully participate in 
electoral politics. The second one is, strengthen laws against criminalisation, barring 
candidates with serious charges, especia lly heinous crimes an d hate speech from 
contesting elections until cleared by courts.  Reform the appointment process of the 
Election Commission, freeing it from excessi ve executive interfer ence and ensuring a 
transparent, bipartisan mechanism that inspires public confidence.  
Sir, democracy cannot survive on rituals alone. It survives on trust, fairness 
and representation. …(Time-Bell rings.)…  Electoral reforms are not a partisan 
demand, they are a necessity for us today.  If the vote is weakened, the Republic is 
weakened. Let us act before the right to vote  stops meaning anything.  Thank  
you, Sir.

DR. SASMI
T PATRA (Odisha) :  Hon.  Vice- Chairman Sir, I have 120 seconds, two 
minutes, to be precise, and I will be quick. It is not your concern; it is my party, which 
has given me a total of two minutes.  
 On behalf of our hon. L eader, Shri Naveen Patnaik ji, and my party, the Biju 
Janata Dal, I place before this august House ten specific suggestions on election 
reforms, very succinctly, within the next 1. 5 minutes:   First, real-time, booth-level 
data publication and authentication; seco nd reform, a harmonised reconciliation 
system for simultaneous polls; third reform, standardised report ing of extended-hour 
polling percentages; fourth reform, auto matic sharing of Form 17C and turnout 
records with all concerned political parties;  fifth reform, full VVPAT-EVM matching at 
every booth to create verifiable trust; si xth reform, time-bound disposal of election 
representation and objections; seventh re form, independent third-party audits of 
critical election processes; eighth reform, pre-declared border management and 
inter-State coordination in sensitive consti tuencies; ninth reform, strengthen training 
and monitoring for polling personnel to uphold voter secrecy and procedural 
discipline; and, the last and the tenth re form, creation of an integrated election 
transparency dashboard.

161

 
 
Hon. Vice-Chairman Sir, these reforms are not rooted in criticism, but in 
experience and evidence of our party, the Biju Janata Dal's on-ground observations, 
our extensive submissions to the Election Commission.  On behalf of our hon. 
Leader, Shri Naveen Patnaik;  our party, the Biju Janata  Dal; and, the people of 
Odisha, I submit these reforms for the constructive and purposeful development of 
India's electoral democracy. With your permi ssion, I would like to lay the rest of my 
speech on the table. 
THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (Shri Bhubaneswar Kalita) : You can take one or two minutes 
more.   
DR. SASMIT PATRA:   Okay, I am grateful, hon. Vice-Chairman Sir.  
THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (Shri Bhubaneswar Kalita): Please read your suggestions.  
DR. SASMIT PATRA:  First and foremost, I will first co me to the first reform, and you 
can stop me whenever it is appropriate. In  the real-time booth-level data publication 
and authentication, in 2024, several booths reflected discrepancies between reported 
turnout Form 17C entries and EVM vote counts. To prevent ambiguity of this nature, a 
mandatory system of real-time publication of booth-level turnout and counting data, 
digitally authenticated and publicly accessi ble, would ensure that data cannot be 
altered, delayed or withheld. This is the single most effective step towards 
transparency.  The second point is, a harmonized reconciliation system for 
simultaneous polls. Our review  of constituencies with simultaneous Lok Sabha and 
Assembly elections shows mismatch be tween both despite identical voter 
participation.  A unified, automated re conciliation protocol – from booth to 
constituency – would ensure that simultaneo us poll data is consistent.  Thirdly, I 
request a standardized reporting of ex tended-hour polling percentages. In 2024, 
several constituencies witnessed late surges in final polling percentages that were not 
explained clearly or uniformly. To address th is, a structured protocol is needed.  
Wherever polling extends beyond scheduled hours, a formal explanation, booth-wise 
data release, and time-stamped reporting sh ould be made mandatory. This will help 
build clarity and prevent speculation.  
 On the issue of automatic sharing of Form 17C and turnout records with all 
political parties, one of the persistent concerns that has been raised by our party, in 
our delegations that met the Election Co mmission several times, was about the 
delayed or incomplete availability of Form  17C across several booths.  Making the 
digital Form 17C automatically available to all recognized political parties immediately 
162
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

after polling would end recurring contestation over turnout data and ensure uniform 
access to authenticated records.  
On the full VVPAT-EVM matching at every booth to create verifiable trust,  hon. 
Vice-Chairman, Sir,  across the 2024 Gene ral Elections and Nuapada by-poll, 
stakeholders repeatedly sought verification through VVPAT systems.   A 100 per cent 
VVPAT count- rather than selective samplin g – would establish incontrovertible trust 
in electronic voting.  The technology and manpower exist; what is needed is the 
institutional decision to adopt full verificat ion as a standard practice for strengthening 
democratic confidence.  
Hon. Vice-Chairman, Sir, on time-bound disposal of election representations 
and objections: during the 2024 polls and recently on the by-poll as well, many 
representations were submitted to the Chief Electoral Officer or the Election 
Commission including urgent-day-of-poll appeals, which remained pending beyond a 
period in which action would have mattered.    Introducing a statutory timeframe for 
addressing representations – with acknowledgement, action taken and reasons 
recorded would deepen accountability in electoral administration.  
One of the key aspects, hon. Vice-Chairma n, Sir, is to strengthen training and 
monitoring – I will close with this; this is last, please – for polling personnel to uphold 
voters’ secrecy and procedural discipline.  Recently during a by -poll in Nuapada, 
a Presiding Officer was suspended for violat ing voter secrecy.  This underscores the 
need for rigorous and continuous training .  Standardized refresher courses, live 
simulation exercises and real-time supervi sory checks are necessary; that will ensure 
that every polling staff member upholds procedural integrity without exception. 
Sir, I thank you for your time and I hope that these reforms will go a long way in 
strengthening Indian democracy.    Thank you, Sir.

legality, transparency and neutrality. The Opposition claims that ECI has no legal 
166
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
justification to conduct the SIR. सर , एसआईआर क्या है?  It is simply a voter list 
verification process. बोगस वोटसर् को िलÎट से बाहर करना और genuine वोटसर् को िलÎट मȂ 
लाना।  This is simply what the SIR is.  Article 324 of the Constitution and Section 21(3) 
of the Representation of People’s Act, 1 951, mandates the ECI to do this.  So, the 
SIR is simply an exercise of this legal authority given by the Constitution.  
 Sir, free and fair election has been in grained into the Basic Structure of the 
Constitution, as articulated by the hon. Supreme Court in the Keshavanand Bharti  
judgement of 1973.  Accuracy of the electora l roll is one of the ke y essentials for free 
and fair elections.  So, if ghuspaithiya and illegal voters cast their votes, then, the 
elections are meaningless and that is violative of the Basic Structure.                                  
This is why SIR is legal and should be conducted at regular intervals. SIR is 
being done in the most transparent manner.  Voters have to verify their inclusions in 
the rolls and they can raise claims for co rrection or objection from the period of 
publication of draft rolls to the release of final rolls. सर, इितहास गवाह है िक िरयल वोट
